## No Code Is Code: Zapier CEO Wade Foster on Headless Tools, Zapier MCP &amp; Automation Bench
Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2ayx_3mHPgU
Published: 2026-09-17T13:27:46+00:00
Type: channel video (last 26h)

Hello and welcome back to the Cognitive Revolution. Today I'm excited to welcome Wade Foster, co-founder and CEO of Zapier, back to the show. When I last spoke to Wade in September of 2024, some 400,000 customers had already used Zapier to delegate more than 100 million tasks to AI. And YC President Gary Tan was calling Zapier the AI powered knowledge worker of the future. Since then, models have of course become dramatically more capable and Zapier has built out a full AI portfolio, including agents, chatbots, an MCP server, an SDK, and an AI guardrails product. And yet, somehow, white collar work and the world as a whole have changed much less than I would have expected. With that in mind, I wanted to hear not only about what Zapier has built and how it's continued to evolve as a company, but what Wade and team have learned about how businesses across the economy understand and use today's AI tools. At a high level, Wade believes that most people are now settling in to using a single daily driver, whether that's Cloud Code, ChatgPT, Grockbot, or in WDE's case, Cursor, and that platforms like Zapier will need to adapt by making their tools available and effective in those environments. Practically, he observes that models still struggle with many business tasks, as illustrated by Astra, setting a new high of just 40% success on Zapier's automation bench, which consists of roughly 600 knowledge work tasks across marketing, sales, HR, and operations. He also argues that many tasks that people are delegating to AI would be better done with deterministic code and that for a while longer at least there is therefore tremendous ROI to time invested in structuring and validating workflows. We then go on to discuss what Zapier is doing to help people recognize exactly what AI might be able to do for them. starting with his own weekly automation, which Wade says they will soon productize for customers that reviews his activity across Gmail, Slack, the browser, cursor, and more, and then proposes specific tools and workflows that he should be building. We also talk about how Zapier is implementing recursive self-improvement loops internally and how much value they're finding in running multiple different AIs on the same problem. Why Wade chose to put Zapier's chief people officer in charge of AI transformation but wouldn't necessarily recommend that strategy to other companies. How Zapier is moving toward public bydefault communications to make more and more context available to AIS. Why they still don't limit individuals use of AI but have created dashboards to help employees better understand and manage their own usage. how Zapier, which holds a huge number of highv value user credentials, is thinking about security in the context of rapidly rising cyber security risks. And finally, how Wade thinks about co-authorship between humans and AIs, with the upshot being that he believes individuals should use AI to help improve their writing and shouldn't be afraid of being panggrammed, but also that it's critical that people be prepared to explain and stand behind the work that they ship. With that, I hope you enjoy this very grounded and highly practical conversation about making AI automation work for people outside the AI bubble with WDE Foster, co-founder and CEO of Zapier. The Cognitive Revolution is brought to you by Mercury, the fintech that more than 300,000 ambitious companies and individuals trust to run their finances. I've wired AI into nearly every corner of my life. my email, my messages, my calendar. I even gave Mercury virtual cards to my agents with low limits and category and merchant restrictions for their autonomous use. But still, my AI's access to my financial data has remained limited. With a normal bank, I might export a bunch of statements and have my assistant process them for me. But for real-time up-to-date information and certainly for taking any action, trying to get your agent to use the bank via the browser is just too hard, too slow, and too errorprone to be worth it. And that's why Mercury's new conversational interface command is such a big deal. It's built directly into Mercury, which means you get natural language access to your finances without exposing anything outside of your bank account. No exports, no spreadsheets, no pasting your transactions into thirdparty tools. I really think a lot of people are going to prefer it this way. And it can already help you take actions too with everything bound by the permissions and approval policies that you've already set up in your account. I am genuinely impressed to see this level of AI integration in banking in 2026. And so I invite you to join me in the future. Visit mercury.com to learn more and apply online in minutes. Mercury is a fintech company, not an FDIC insured bank. Banking services provided through Choice Financial Group and Column NA members FDIC. Thank you to Mercury for supporting the Cognitive Revolution. And now on with the show. Wade Foster, CEO of Zapier. Welcome back to the Cognitive Revolution. >> Yeah, thanks for having me again, Nathan. Boy, what a difference a not super long period of time makes. I, you know, I can't believe it. Every time I have a returning guest, it's an opportunity to look back and look at what the state of AI was, models, outlooks, you know, what the what the possibilities were, what the capabilities were at the time. And suffice it to say, obviously, a lot has changed. We only have one hour today, so I'm going to try to discipline myself, not talk too much, and give you most of the air time. I would love to start off with just first of all an observation that like many ambitious software companies you guys at Zapier have been really prolific and I would say you've kind of built everything in the sense that you now have in addition to workflows which of course can call out to AIS you've got agents you've got chat bots you've got an MCP you've got an SDK you've got a guardrails product and probably more you know that I didn't even notif identify or mention what have you learned by building all that stuff like what has taken off and resonated maybe better than you thought, what has been slower than you thought, and what's the sort of synthesis view that you have informed by these different product efforts and their relative successes? >> Yeah. Well, I mean, shoot, I was on 18 months ago, and if I think back to that time, what I don't think we knew yet is what the shape of these AI experiences that we're going to take off. ## @emollick — post
Source: https://x.com/emollick/status/2100665801800093959
Published: 2026-09-17T19:18:50+00:00
Type: X post (last 26h)

What makes Claude Projects so interesting is that it handles teams of agents really well, you talk to a main orchestrator agent and it spins up specialists. Basically it creates an organization to solve your issue, mixing expensive and cheap agents depending on your preferences.

For example, I asked Fable in Claude Projects to select famous historical mysteries that it could try to resolve. It initiated research agents, selected the mysteries based on data it could access, and spun up eighteen separate threads, each with an agent each focused on one mystery. Then each thread launched additional agents (simulating avalanches, breaking codes) before summarizing those and passing them to still more agents for write up and another set of skeptical agents to fact check. It did this over a day of work, with the central orchestrator agent organizing it all. 

The results were interesting if you like historical mysteries. They are also for fun and certainly not definitive or guaranteed error-free (but they are also mostly reasonable & grounded in the literature). https://t.co/uHToT5GNuF

## @EugenioFierro3 — post

Goodwipes turns OpenAI's Astra ad into Asstra

When OpenAI unveiled its ad for the Astra model, wet-wipe brand Goodwipes spotted a cultural opening and turned it into a bathroom-humor parody built almost entirely with generative AI.

🚽 The project, called Asstra, mirrors the original spot's scenes of people giving voice commands to an AI assistant, then reveals they're actually sitting on toilets. Built with AI-native agency OK Future, the joke rests on a familiar Goodwipes idea: tech moves fast, bathroom habits barely change.

💡 The real value here isn't just speed, it's recognition. The joke only works if audiences connect Astra's polished visuals with Goodwipes' reveal. OK Future's founder said a traditional process would have taken months to sell the idea, secure budget and shoot it, long enough for the cultural window to close. Generative tools compressed that into days.

⚙️ Speed didn't remove craft. The workflow still involved character studies, storyboards and set design, just without a physical set. AI added its own friction too: it kept placing people on closed toilet seats, a detail essential to the joke, and packaging came out with wrong lids or garbled text, forcing extra rounds of review.

🎯 Those packaging details mattered because they were brand assets, not finishing touches. Goodwipes' marketing lead accepted some imperfections in the final cut, since the rough edges actually fit the campaign's light, humorous tone. Not a quality compromise, but a deliberate call on what the brand could tolerate.

Why it matters: this reframes what brand guidelines are for. In an AI-assisted workflow they can't just be a final check, they need to shape prompts and selection criteria from the start. The question isn't whether AI can produce polished ads anymore, it's whether a brand's identity is concrete enough to guide production this fast.

What strikes me most is how this shifts the conversation from who generates the most assets to who stays recognizable while doing it in record time. I keep seeing brands rush an AI trend without that discipline, and it shows immediately, the output looks generic next to something with a real point of view. Goodwipes had a clear product truth and packaging cues going in, and that's what gave the experiment direction instead of noise.

Carl's Jr. Turns A $5.99 Burger Into An AI-Powered Epic

Carl's Jr. just dropped a gladiator-style spot for its new Angus Maximus burger, and the real story isn't only the packed Colosseum. The blue-screen scenes behind that spectacle were built using AI techniques the creative team had never used before, mixed with real food and real actors shot live.

🍔 For the 'Angus Maximus' campaign, Carl's Jr. teamed up with agency Erich & Kallman, who did more than shoot an ad: they also named the new $5.99 burger itself (⅓ lb of Angus beef, melted American cheese, onions, pickles and special sauce on a toasted brioche bun). The result is a spectacle worthy of an emperor, with a Roman gladiator riling up a packed arena.

🎯 According to Burge Diemer, CMO at Carl's Jr., the spot introduces the cooked-to-order burger as a flavor-forward alternative at a key moment: it's the brand's first limited-time offer built to spotlight its shift toward cooked-to-order preparation across the board, a change Diemer frames as central to the brand's broader revolution this year.

🎬 On the production side, the Erich & Kallman crew shot all of the epic scenes on blue screen using a variety of AI techniques that were new to the team, while the food and the actors were captured live. Eric Kallman, Co-Founder and CCO of the agency, describes the burger and the campaign as confident and irreverent, calling the spot an epic proclamation reflective of the brand's longstanding swagger.

💡 Why it matters: this is another sign that AI is becoming a normal part of mainstream advertising production, not as a replacement for actors or real food, but as a way to speed up the most expensive and complex parts of a shoot, like building elaborate blue-screen worlds. For agencies, production houses and brand teams, it's a preview of how workflows are shifting: AI takes over more of the visual heavy lifting, while casting, food styling and creative direction stay firmly human.

What I find genuinely interesting here is that a brand as famously over-the-top as Carl's Jr. chose AI specifically to amplify the excess and spectacle that have always been part of its advertising DNA, and did it openly rather than hiding it, which says a lot about how normalized AI production has become even for brands built on shock value rather than subtlety; it doesn't replace the humans who cook the food or act in the spot, it just changes how the world around them gets built.

## @EugenioFierro3 — post

OpenAI turns ChatGPT into an ad platform with Sponsored Agents

OpenAI is testing a new ad format inside ChatGPT called Sponsored Agents. The concept is simple but significant: after seeing a relevant ad, users can choose to start a conversation with a business-sponsored agent right inside ChatGPT.

🤖 According to OpenAI, the user can explain what matters to them, ask follow-up questions, and follow a link to the business's website when they're ready to take the next step. The company is clear that this conversation is distinct from ChatGPT's independent answers and separate from the original chat the user started.

📢 For now, Sponsored Agents are being tested with select advertisers in the United States only. Worth noting: this is an early, limited experiment, not a global rollout, so it's still early days for the format.

🛠️ Alongside this, OpenAI is upgrading the whole Ads Manager ecosystem. Advertisers will be able to build campaigns by simply writing prompts in ChatGPT Work, new AI creative tools can suggest copy and imagery based on a landing page, and fresh integrations with HubSpot and Shopify let businesses manage product catalogs and leads without leaving those platforms.

📊 There's also a batch of more technical updates for people running campaigns: flexible attribution windows (7, 14, or 30 days on clicks), granular platform targeting across iOS, Android, and web, plus a new Event Quality Score to help advertisers assess the quality of their conversion data setup.

💡 Why it matters: if Sponsored Agents work as described, they could reshape how we think about conversational advertising. Instead of a banner that just pushes traffic somewhere else, this is an interaction that stays inside the app and tries to answer a user's questions on the spot. For businesses, it's a new lead generation channel built directly into the assistant. For anyone working in digital marketing, it's another sign that ad UX is migrating straight into AI chat interfaces.

🧭 My take is that this deserves curiosity but also a bit of caution, because the line between a helpful assistant and a salesperson in disguise is thin, and ChatGPT's credibility rests largely on the perception that its answers are independent; if OpenAI manages to keep that boundary clear, as it claims it will, this could open a genuinely new business model without damaging the core experience, but if that line ever gets blurry the reputational risk is real.

## @EugenioFierro3 — post

Type: X post (last 26h)

How a phone call can quietly block AI regulation

According to The Wall Street Journal, it wasn't a congressional hearing that stopped a proposed independent AI regulator in the US. It was a handful of private calls to Donald Trump.

📞 The story starts with an idea from Demis Hassabis, CEO of Google DeepMind: an independent body modeled on FINRA, the authority that oversees Wall Street, designed to test frontier models for cybersecurity, biological and deception risks up to 30 days before public release, starting on a voluntary basis.

⚖️ Per the Journal's reporting, Meta's Mark Zuckerberg, SpaceXAI's Elon Musk and Nvidia's Jensen Huang each spoke separately with Trump to voice their opposition, reportedly worried the body would end up entrenching more power in the hands of the three labs already leading the race, OpenAI, Anthropic and Google DeepMind. After those calls, the president decided not to move forward with the proposal.

🎬 Meanwhile, David Sacks, Trump's former AI and crypto czar, is pushing a lighter alternative: an industry-led body modeled on the Motion Picture Association, the organization behind film ratings like PG-13 and R. Sacks has dismissed the idea of a government regulator as 'a DMV for AI', where models line up waiting for their test.

🏛️ This isn't the first time a single phone call has redirected policy. Back in May, Sacks reportedly convinced Trump to shelve a broader AI executive order the same morning it was set to be signed, catching Chief of Staff Susie Wiles and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent off guard. They later pushed through a scaled-back version, driven partly by concerns that AI-powered cyberattacks could hit the banking system.

Why it matters: for anyone building with AI, this story says something important about how the rules of the game are actually being written. The policies that will decide which models reach the market, under what checks and on what timeline, aren't only shaped in public hearings or technical white papers, they're also shaped by who has direct access to the president's phone. For startups, developers and companies without that kind of access, it means competing on a field where the rules can shift overnight, based on conversations nobody outside the room ever sees.

💭 What strikes me most in this story is not a simple good versus bad narrative. The same executives asking for lighter rules are often the ones who already dominate the market, and the line between corporate interest and public interest gets very thin very fast. Working with these tools every day, I keep coming back to one thought: the speed at which these decisions get made deserves at least the same transparency we expect from the models themselves.

A full feature film generated shot by shot by a single AI model

Kling AI has announced that 'Odysseus: The Fall', the new feature from Fountain 0 directed by Ash Koosha, is now available in full, with every single shot generated exclusively by Kling 3.0.

🎬 This isn't a demo reel or a proof of concept. According to Fountain 0, it's a 150-minute feature film retelling Homer's Odyssey, with Koosha himself lending his likeness to play Odysseus. Co-founder Pooya Koosha explained that the studio tested a range of AI video models before settling on Kling AI for its stronger control and consistency, which he says let Ash direct the film in a way that felt closer to working on a traditional production set.

🧠 This is the same team behind 'Dreams of Violets', the AI-generated film that premiered at the Tribeca Festival and already put Fountain 0 on the map as one of the first studios to bring a fully AI-generated production to a major festival stage. With Odysseus, the ambition scales up: a budget reportedly in the mid-five figures, three months of part-time solo work, and a result the studio compares to the quality of a big-budget Hollywood movie.

⚙️ On the technical side, Kling 3.0 brings native multilingual audio, stronger subject consistency across scenes, and multi-shot generation, features that Kuaishou says allow creators to build complex narrative sequences instead of stitching together isolated clips. That's exactly the kind of backbone a full-length film needs, rather than a handful of experimental scenes.

💡 Why it matters: if a single director really can write, direct and produce a two-and-a-half-hour film on an indie-short budget, it shifts who gets to tell cinematic stories at scale. For solo creators and small studios, it means competing on vision and storytelling without chasing studio-sized budgets. For the industry, it's a real-world test of how much audiences will accept a film that's 'directed' more than it's 'shot'.

🤔 What strikes me most here isn't really the technology itself, it's how quickly the idea of a total author-director, someone who writes, directs and generates every frame alone, is becoming normalized: to me it feels less like a threat to Hollywood and more like a return to artisanal auteur cinema, though it's still an open question whether audiences will reward Koosha's personal vision as much as curiosity carried Dreams of Violets.
